From the 1950s onward, Mandarin was established as the national standard language and introduced into the education system. A second phase, beginning in the early 1980s, required the use of Mandarin in classrooms and encouraged its use beyond formal settings, substantially increasing everyday exposure (Zhou, 2001). A third phase, initiated in the mid-1990s, introduced standardised proficiency testing for professionals in education, media, and government, further reinforcing the role of Standard Mandarin in public and institutional domains (P. Chen, 1999). As a result, younger speakers in mainland China (thus in Quanzhou) have experienced more consistent and intensive exposure to Mandarin than older generations, who were primarily socialised in a QSM-dominant environment (Weng et al., 2026).

Thus, these two contrast mergers provide a useful testing ground for examining how bilingual speakers represent and realize phonological distinctions that are absent from their first language. They also allow us to investigate whether perception and production show parallel patterns across contrasts, or whether outcomes diverge depending on their phonetic properties and sociophonetic embedding.

1.3.2. /f/~/x/ contrast merger

The merger of labial and velar fricatives has been documented in a range of linguistic communities, arising through both historical sound change and synchronic contact-induced variation. In the history of Spanish, Latin /f/ shifted to /h/ and subsequently to /x/ or ∅ (Penny, 2000), illustrating how labial and velar fricatives can converge through natural phonetic pathways over time. Perceptual factors contribute to this convergence: Greenlee (1992) demonstrated that speakers of Chicano Spanish made more perceptual confusions between /f/ and /x/ in the context of a following rounded vowel, since both fricatives produce more similar formant transitions into rounded vowels, reducing discriminability. The presence of a labial glide similarly promotes confusion. Ohala and Lorentz (1977) showed that when [w] occurs adjacent to a velar fricative, its labial articulation is perceptually salient, yielding a labial percept such as [f]. Mazzaro (2011) confirmed this pattern for Argentine Spanish, showing lower discrimination accuracy for /f/ and /x/ when the velar fricative was followed by [w].

In bilingual and bidialectal settings, the merger manifests as a contact-induced production phenomenon. Peng (1993) examined the production of Mandarin /f/ and /x/ by three groups of native Taiwanese Southern Min (TSM) speakers differentiated by Mandarin proficiency, comparing them to Mandarin monolinguals. She found that the least proficient speakers simply substituted their native TSM phonemes /hw/ and /h/ for Mandarin /f/ and /x/ respectively, while intermediate speakers could approximate the Mandarin norm for the phonetically new phoneme /f/ more accurately than for the similar phoneme /x/: a pattern consistent with the greater difficulty of reorganizing a category that already has a native language equivalent. Only the most proficient speakers showed little cross-language interference on either contrast, though they exhibited some reverse influence of Mandarin /x/ on their TSM production. A comparable pattern of context-conditioned merger has been documented in Zhongjiang Southwestern Mandarin (Rao & Shaw, 2021), where acoustic analysis reveals that the velar fricative /x/ shifts spectrally toward /f/ in labialised environments: the spectral center of gravity of /x/ is raised when followed by labialization (e.g., rounding of a following vowel), producing fricative tokens that overlap phonetically with /f/. This demonstrates how coarticulation with a following labial element creates gradient merger pressure on the velar category. In the QSM-Mandarin community specifically, Weng et al. (2023) investigated the perceptual side of this variation, showing that QSM-Mandarin bilinguals exhibit significantly lower discrimination accuracy for the /f/~/x/ contrast in the context of the rounded vowel [u] and the labial glide [w] compared to when they were followed by the vowel [a]. Individual-level exposure to Mandarin was found to modulate perceptual performance, with higher-exposure speakers showing reduced accuracy differences across phonological contexts.

Socially, the /f/∼/x/ merger is evaluated as a regional feature. It has been reported to carry stigma, being associated with rural background and limited education (Peng, 1993; Rao & Shaw, 2021). However, it is readily recognisable to listeners familiar with Fujian, as the Hujianese stereotype noted above illustrates. It does not figure in the national discourse about what counts as correct Putonghua. More recently it has also been reinterpreted locally, with puns based on the merger circulating on social media and on local signage, which present a formerly stigmatised pronunciation as a marker of Quanzhou identity. This development has taken place in a period in which local cultural heritage has been actively promoted, and in which the city was named a UNESCO World Heritage site in 2021. The coexistence of negative and positive evaluations is not unusual. Dong (2010) reports an interviewee who describes Putonghua as awkward and unnatural at home but neutral and powerful in public. Zhao and Liu (2021) find that the same regional variety of Putonghua attracts both negative cultural stereotypes and positive claims of local authenticity, and that a devalued regional variety can be revalued locally as a marker of authenticity and local belonging. The merger can therefore carry both stigma and local value, and in either case what it indexes is regional belonging rather than proximity to the standard.

1.3.3. /s/∼/ù/ contrast merger

The /s/∼/ù/ merger has been extensively documented in Southern Min-Mandarin contact varieties. While early accounts attributed the merger primarily to L1 transfer (Kubler, 1985) given the absence of retroflex sibilants in Southern Min, more recent work suggests that the phenomenon cannot be explained solely by cross-linguistic influence and instead reflects socially-conditioned and speaker-specific variability (Y.-h. S. Chang & Shih, 2015; Chuang & Fon, 2010; Lee-Kim & Chou, 2022; Lee-Kim & Tung, 2025). A detailed review of the /s/∼/ù/ merger in production is provided in Weng et al. (2026).

Both production and perception of this sibilant contrast have been investigated in previous work. Production studies consistently show that the contrast is variably realised rather than categorically neutralised. Rates of merger are conditioned by social factors such as gender and age: male and older speakers tend to exhibit higher merger rates, whereas younger speakers more frequently maintain a clearer /s/∼/ù/ distinction, often aligning with standardised Mandarin norms (Chuang & Fon, 2010; Lee-Kim & Chou, 2022). Similar generational differentiation has also been reported in other Mandarin contact varieties (Beckman, Li, Kong, & Edwards, 2014; Y. Lin, 2018). These patterns align with sociolinguistic accounts suggesting that women are more likely to adopt standard linguistic forms due to socially mediated gender roles (Gal, 1978; Labov, 2001; Stuart-Smith, 2020).

Perceptual studies likewise reveal gradient sensitivity to the contrast, though the extent to which perceptual categorization aligns with individual production patterns varies across speakers and communities (e.g., Lee-Kim & Tung, 2023; Y. Lin et al., 2021). These findings indicate that the /s/∼/ù/ contrast occupies an unstable position in the phonological system: it is neither fully preserved nor uniformly merged, but instead shows socially stratified and individually variable realizations. This instability makes it a particularly informative case for examining how perception and production interact under conditions of phonological variation in societal bilingual communities.

The social meaning of the sibilant merger is of a different kind. The opposition between the alveolar and the retroflex sibilants is the main feature by which standard pronunciation is recognised in China, and it is explicitly taught, corrected and tested (Dong, 2010, p. 271). Since the retroflex series is absent from many southern Sinitic varieties and from the Mandarin varieties in contact with them, including those of Shanghai, Zhejiang, Fujian, Guangdong and Hong Kong, as well as Taiwan and Singapore (e.g., Beckman et al., 2014; Chiu, Wei, Noguchi, & Yamane, 2019; Goh et al., 2024; Weng et al., 2026), its merger is heard as a general southern accent rather than as a feature of the Fujian region. Variation in this contrast is thus evaluated along a standard versus non-standard dimension, whereas variation in /f/∼/x/ is evaluated along a regional dimension.

1.4. Research questions and hypotheses

While speech perception and production are widely assumed to be linked, it remains unclear whether, within the same bilingual community, the perception-production relationship is expressed uniformly across all contrasts, or whether it is modulated by contrast-specific properties and by speakers' individual usage of each language, as well as by their generation. The present study addresses this question in the QSM-Mandarin bilingual community, through two Mandarin fricative contrasts that differ in their phonological, phonetic and sociolinguistic properties.

The two contrasts are also expected to differ in perceptual difficulty, and we use the Perceptual Assimilation Model for second-language learners (PAM-L2; Best & Tyler, 2007) to make this expectation explicit. PAM-L2 predicts the discriminability of a non-native contrast from the way its members are assimilated to the categories of the native inventory. Under Two-Category assimilation, the two sounds are assimilated to two distinct native categories, and discrimination is expected to be accurate; under Category-Goodness assimilation, both are assimilated to the same native category but one is a better exemplar of it than the other, and discrimination is expected to be moderate; under Single-Category assimilation, both are assimilated to the same native category with comparable goodness of fit, and discrimination is expected to be poor. We adopt PAM-L2 rather than PAM (Best, 1995), since the latter concerns listeners with no prior experience of the target language, whereas our participants are early bilinguals in a long-standing contact setting. The model is used here to motivate the choice of contrasts and to provide a perceptual baseline, rather than as a framework for the perception-production relationship itself. 1QSM lacks both members of the non-sibilant contrast, so that its listeners are expected to assimilate them to the native fricative space, which in this region contains the glottal /h/ and the labialized /h w /. Before the unrounded vowel [a], the two Mandarin fricatives plausibly map onto two distinct native forms: /f/, whose labiality is realised as labialization, is assimilated to /h w /, while /x/ is assimilated to /h/. Before the rounded vowel [u], the rounding of the vowel imposes labialization on both, so that the two are expected to converge on a single native form. The mapping for this contrast is therefore expected to depend on vowel context.

For the sibilant contrast, QSM has no retroflex category, so that both Mandarin /s/ and Mandarin /ù/ are expected to be assimilated to QSM /s/, in both vowel contexts; no vowel can supply a second category for /ù/. 2 This does not mean that vowel context leaves the sibilants acoustically unchanged. The high back rounded vowel [u] coarticulates with a preceding sibilant, and both its lip rounding and its tongue-body retraction lower the spectral centre of gravity of the frication, so that [sū] is acoustically somewhat more /ù/like than [sā]. This coarticulation, however, shifts both members of the contrast in the same direction and towards the same native category, and so does not provide /ù/ with an anchor that /s/ lacks. The mapping for this contrast is therefore expected to be the same in both vowel contexts.

The assimilation type associated with each mapping is given in Table 2, and two ordinal expectations follow. First, /s/∼/ù/ should be discriminated less accurately than /f/∼/x/ overall, since only the latter involves a Two-Category mapping in one of the two vowel contexts, whereas the sibilants are assimilated to a single native category throughout. Second, vowel context should affect /f/∼/x/ more than /s/∼/ù/, since only /f/∼/x/ involves a mapping that changes with rounding. Because these classifications are hypotheses based on the two inventories rather than measured assimilation patterns, we derive no expectations regarding absolute accuracy, which would require the categorisation and goodness-rating data described above. Where more than one assimilation type is compatible with the mapping, the candidates differ in the absolute accuracy they predict but not in the ordinal expectations derived in the text.

These expectations are not intended as direct hypotheses about production, but rather as a perceptual baseline against which contrast-specific patterns in perception, production, and their relationship can be evaluated. On the basis of these considerations, the study addresses the following research questions: • RQ1: Within a community exhibiting contrast instability, do individual bilingual speakers show a systematic relationship between their perceptual discrimination and their production patterns?

• RQ2: Does the strength of the perception-production relationship differ across two merged Mandarin fricative contrasts (/f/∼/x/ and /s/∼/ù/)?

• RQ3: To what extent is the perception-production relationship modulated by individual differences in language experience (e.g., Mandarin usage, generation)?

Hypotheses

• H1: Perception and production will be positively correlated across speakers. Speakers who distinguish a contrast more reliably in one modality will also do so in the other.

• H2: The perception-production relationship will differ across the two contrasts. For the more merged contrast (i.e., /s/∼/ù/), perception and production may have dissociated, thus weakening the correlation. For the less merged contrast (i.e., /f/∼/x/), the two measures are more likely to covary across speakers.

• H3: The perception-production relationship will be modulated by language experience. Speakers with greater Mandarin language experience will show a weaker perception-production relation, as higher proficiency may preserve perceptual sensitivity while production remains governed by community norms favoring the merged form, leading to a dissociation between the two.

2. Method

The study employed a multi-task design to investigate speech perception and production in QSM-Mandarin bilinguals. Production and perception were examined using complementary tasks that allow both community-level and individual-level variability to be assessed. The experimental design consisted of one production task and one perception task, each targeting the same set of Mandarin fricative contrastsfoot_2.

2.1. Production task

In the production component, speech was elicited using a sentence reading task in which target words were embedded in the same carrier phrase "请阅读单词 X 八遍", "Please read the word X eight times". All target items were real Mandarin words of the form CVCV, 4 constructed by crossing four target fricatives with two vowel contexts ([a] vs. [u]; the vowel [i] was not included as neither/fi/ /xi/ /si/ nor /ùi/ are phonotactically well-formed in Mandarin). There were three items per condition (4 fricatives × 2 vowels × 3 items). The target words all contained a high level tone (Tone 1) on the first syllable.

All target words were presented orthographically using two Simplified Chinese characters (see Table 3). To control for lexical frequency effects, the frequency of each word was matched across conditions and fell within a log frequency range of 3-5 according to the SUBTLEX-CH corpus (Cai & Brysbaert, 2010). Prior to the reading task, participants were presented with a list of all characters appearing in the experimental sentences. They were asked to confirm that they were familiar with each character and to consult the experimenter in case of uncertainty.

contrast fricative context items

Table 3. Mandarin /f/∼/x/ and /s/∼/ù/ stimuli (real words), with each word presented in Chinese characters followed by their corresponding Pinyin in parentheses.

2.2. Perception task

Speech perception was assessed using an ABX discrimination task. In this task, participants heard sequences of three stimuli separated by an inter-stimulus interval of 300 ms. The first two stimuli were different from each other, while the third stimulus (X) was a token either of the first stimulus (A) or the second stimulus (B). The participants' task was to determine whether X was a token of A or of B. The A and B tokens were produced by two male speakers, while X was produced by a female speaker. Using different voices ensured that the decision was not based solely on the acoustic similarity of the A and B tokens to X. The stimuli in the perception task were ten disyllabic nonce words of the form CVCV, constructed from real word syllables. The initial consonant was drawn from the set {/f/, /x/, /s/, /ù/, /m/, /p/}. The contrast of /m/∼/p/ in the context of [a] was also included as a control contrast since this contrast is present in both QSM and Mandarin. The complete set of items is shown in table 4. All of the stimuli were realised with high-level tone (tone

Non-word items used for the target contrasts /f/∼/x/ and /s/∼/ù/, the control contrast /m/∼/p/ and their respective perceptual assimilation predictions 1) on both syllables. All ended with the same second syllable (-tā: 搭), and could be rep-resented orthographically as two simplified Chinese characters. The stimuli were recorded by three L1 speakers of Standard Mandarin (two men and one woman) who grew up in Beijing or in Hebei province, where both target contrasts are robustly maintained. Each item was embedded in the carrier sentence 请阅读单词 X 八遍 and read three times in a sound-attenuated booth. The second reading was selected for the experiment, since the first contained more pauses and the third was sometimes rushed. The stimuli were aligned with the Montreal Forced Aligner (McAuliffe, Socolof, Mihuc, Wagner, & Sonderegger, 2017), checked and corrected manually in Praat, and normalised for peak amplitude. Thus, in one trial, a participant might hear /fātā /M1-/xātā/M2-/fātā/F1, to which they should respond "A". In another trial, they might hear /fātā/M1-/xātā/M2-/xātā/F1, to which they should respond "B". The A and B tokens of a trial were produced by two male talkers and the X token by a female talker. Participant's perceptual decision could therefore not be based on the acoustic similarity between X and one of the two preceding tokens. After each trial, participants were given 3000 ms to respond before the next trial was presented. With one repetition, a total of 288 trials for three contrasts for the main test sessions were created by combining the stimuli into ABA, ABB, BAA, and BAB trials. The order of all trials was randomised. All participants started with a familiarization session using different stimuli than the test session. During this session, they received feedback on whether the response they gave was correct or not. There were three self-timed breaks in the main test session. During each break, the experimenter briefly interacted with the participant, asking how they were feeling and encouraging them to take sufficient rest before continuing.

At the end of the perception task, bilingual participants completed a post-task questionnaire about their language background, age, and how frequently they used Mandarin and QSM during their childhood and currently in interactions with family, friends, and colleagues.

All production and perception stimuli and anonymised coded data (not recordings) along with an R markdown notebook are available for download at the following OSF repository: https://osf.io/3n2dh/overview?view_only=f2aa078126be4010891f40e2fdf8e5ef

2.3. Participants

Sixty-one bilingual speakers of QSM and Mandarin (29 men, 32 women) were recruited in Quanzhou, China. All participants reported native-level fluency in both QSM and Mandarin. They had spent their childhood in Quanzhou and were living there at the time of testing.

Participants reported their age using predefined ranges in the questionnaire rather than as a continuous variable, reflecting the questionnaire design. They were grouped into two categories: younger generation (18-30 years, 27 participants, born 1990-2004) and older generation (31-55 years, 34 participants, born 1968-1991). Age was therefore treated as a categorical variable following the apparent-time approach commonly used in studies of sound change in progress, where differences between age groups are interpreted as reflecting different stages of linguistic change (Labov, 1994). Previous research on the perceptionproduction relationship has adopted this approach to examine whether speakers who participate more strongly in an ongoing change also exhibit differences in the alignment between perception and production (Harrington et al., 2012).

The boundary between the two groups approximately separates speakers whose primary schooling fell after the mid-1990s proficiency-testing reforms from those whose schooling preceded them. Because policy roll-out was gradual and individual exposure varies, this boundary is necessarily approximate; we therefore interpret group differences as cohortlevel tendencies in exposure to Standard Mandarin rather than as sharp individual discontinuities. In Mandarin-speaking communities, several studies have reported age-related differ-ences in the realization of sibilant contrasts. Younger speakers, who have had more consistent exposure to Standard Mandarin through post-reform schooling, tend to produce more distinct Mandarin fricative contrasts, whereas older speakers, socalised in more dialectdominant environments, more frequently exhibit merged or reduced contrasts (Beckman et al., 2014; Y. Lin, 2023). These patterns have been attributed to the increasing influence of Mandarin education and language planning policies. In particular, the mandated use of Pinyin romanization in elementary education and its widespread use in digital input systems reinforce phonemic distinctions at the lexical level and increase speakers' awareness of regional variation in Mandarin (Y. Lin, 2018). Consequently, younger participants in the present sample were more likely to have received formal schooling primarily in Mandarin, whereas older participants were educated prior to the widespread implementation of these policies.

2.4. Procedure

The presentation of the stimuli and logging of participant responses for both production and perception tasks were carried out with scripts written in jsPsych (De Leeuw, 2015). The experiments were carried out in a quiet room in Quanzhou under booth-quality acoustic conditions. Production speech was recorded using the same equipment and calibration protocol as described in Weng et al. (2026). Speech was captured at a sampling rate of 44.1 kHz using a Neumann TLM102 microphone (Neumann, Berlin, Germany) and a USBPre 2 audio mixer by Sound Devices (Reedsburg, WI, USA). For the perception experiment, stimuli were presented over professional AKG K301 headphones. To minimize background noise during the experiment, we placed a noise-canceling filter (Alctron VB 860, Alctron Electronics Company, Ningbo, China) around the recording setup and installed soundproofing foam on both the window and the door of the room. We also ensured that the noise levels were maintained below -48 dB using a Benetech GM1356 digital sound decibel noise level meter tester (Benetech, Shenzhen, China).

To eliminate any influence from exposure to the bilingual QSM-Mandarin experimenter's Mandarin speech, all verbal communication before, during, and after experimental sessions was strictly limited to QSM. Mandarin was reserved solely as a metalanguage for experimental materials (including on-screen instructions).

The entire experiment took around 45 minutes to complete. Each participant received a compensation of 50 RMB. All participants read and signed consent forms prior to data collection. Before conducting the experiment, ethical approval was obtained at the Université Paris Cité (IRB Number: 00012022-95).

2.5. Data processing 2.5.1. Perception data coding and analysis ABX discrimination accuracy was calculated for each bilingual speaker, for each contrast and vowel context. For each trial, responses were coded as correct (1) or incorrect (0). Trials with no recorded response (96 out of 11,818 trials; 0.8%) were excluded. We then fitted two statistical models to the remaining data. The first compared the two target contrasts to the control contrast (i.e., comparing ABX accuracy on /f/∼/x/ and /s/∼/ù/ versus /m/∼/p/). Contrast was coded with the control contrast /m/∼/p/ as the reference level, so that each coefficient expresses the difference between a target contrast and the control. Because contrast varies within participant, we included it as a by-participant random slope following Barr (2013):

The second model is restricted to the two target contrasts and asks whether discrimination accuracy differs between them and, if so, whether the vowel context modulates that difference. Contrast and vowel enter in full-factorial form, with by-participant random intercept and slopes for contrast, vowel, and their interaction (Barr, 2013), with /f/∼/x/ and the [a] context as reference levels.

glmer(correct ∼ contrast × vowel + (1 + contrast × vowel | participant), family = binomial)

We assessed the need for the contrast × vowel interaction via a likelihood-ratio test against the simpler model including only significant terms without an interaction.

2.5.2. Production data coding and analysis

To obtain an independent and functionally-grounded measure of production, we adopt a perception-centered approach rather than focussing purely on the acoustic signal. In the present study, production is operationalised as intelligibility by L1 Mandarin listeners, rather than as an acoustic measure of the speech signal. Intelligibility was defined as the extent to which intended phonological targets were reliably identified by L1 Mandarin listeners.

A total of 10 L1 Mandarin listeners (five men, five women; aged 18-30, matching the age range of the younger QSM bilingual group) participated in the perceptual evaluation of the bilingual productions. All listeners grew up in northern China, were university students in Paris at the time of the experiment, and had arrived in France within the three previous years. They reported that Mandarin remained an important language in their daily life. Prior to the experiment, the first author conducted an informal conversational screening to ensure that L1 Mandarin listeners maintained clear /f/∼/x/ and /s/∼/ù/ distinctions in their own speech. All listeners consistently produced these contrasts distinctly during the screening.

Intelligibility was calculated as the proportion of tokens correctly identified by the Mandarin listeners, providing a perceptually grounded measure of bilingual production performance. The perceptual judgement task was a two-alternative forced-choice task. From each word produced by the QSM bilinguals, we extracted the target syllable, which was presented in isolation. The two response options were displayed on the screen as a character with its pinyin transcription, and they formed a minimal pair differing only in the target fricative, for example 夫 fū and 呼 hū for a production of /fu/. Each token was judged by all ten raters. Because the stimuli were isolated syllables rather than whole words, listeners could not use the remainder of the word to recover the target. A response was scored as correct when the category chosen by the rater matched the target category of the word read by the speaker. For example, if a bilingual speaker who merges the /f/∼/x/ contrast produces the target syllable 夫 fū and realises it in a way that the rater identifies as 呼 hū, the response is scored as incorrect. Each of the ten raters judged all produced tokens, yielding in total 13,920 judgements.

To statistically evaluate L1 Mandarin raters' perceptual judgments of fricative productions by bilingual QSM-Mandarin speakers, we fitted mixed-effects logistic regression models predicting correct identification responses. The fixed effects included fricative (/f/, /x/, /s/, /ù/), and vowel context ([a] vs. [u]), while random intercepts were included for speaker (QSM-Mandarin bilinguals) and rater (L1 Mandarin raters) to account for variability in production quality and rater response bias. By-speaker and by-rater random slopes for fricative, vowel, and their interaction were included to account for inter-speaker variability in fricative realizations and vowel-dependent effects. The model is as follows:

We additionally checked that L1 listeners' judgments were acoustically anchored, using a between-category Euclidean-distance analysis in the spectral moments space. For /f/∼/x/, distance was computed using the z-scored center of gravity (CoG) and spectral standard deviation; for /s/∼/ù/, using the z-scored CoG and skewness. These correspond with the measures reported to primarily distinguish each fricative pair (Jongman, Wayland, & Wong, 2000; Li, Zhang, & Li, 2014; Peng, 1993; Rao & Shaw, 2021). Greater betweencategory distance predicted higher L1-judged intelligibility for both contrasts, confirming that listeners' judgments track the acoustic properties of the productions. This supports our use of L1-judged identification accuracy as a perceptually grounded measure of production differentiation; full details of the acoustic measurements are provided in Appendix A.

2.5.3. Perception-production link coding and analysis

For the analyses of the perception-production link, both measures were aggregated to one value per speaker × contrast × vowel combination, i.e. one measure of production accuracy and one measure of perception accuracy per speaker for each contrast and each vowel context. Production accuracy per contrast is determined by calculating the mean of the two category-specific identification rates within the contrast; for /s/∼/ù/, for example, it is the mean of the proportion of /s/ tokens identified as /s/ and the proportion of /ù/ tokens identified as /ù/ in each vowel context. Perception accuracy per contrast per vowel context is determined as the speaker's ABX discrimination accuracy.

Social predictors.

We considered two social predictors. Generation is a two-level factor distinguishing younger and older generation bilingual speakers: a variable allowing us to test whether L2 language usage and generation modulate the relationship between perception and production accuracy.

Mandarin-QSM language usage is a continuous score derived from a four-domain questionnaire developed in our earlier work (Weng et al., 2023; Weng, Chitoran, & Martin, 2024). Participants indicated their relative use of Mandarin in (i) their childhood language environment, (ii) family interactions, (iii) communication with friends, and (iv) communication at work or school, on a five-point scale coded -2 (0% Mandarin), -1 (25%), 0 (50%), +1 (75%), and +2 (100% Mandarin), with the complementary proportion attributed to QSM. We summed the four domain scores into a single Mandarin-QSM exposure and use score in [-8, +8]; negative values index QSM dominance, positive values Mandarin dominance, and 0 balanced use. The distribution of this score across participants is shown in Figure 1. The questionnaire did not distinguish Standard Mandarin from the local variety of Mandarin. The two varieties form a continuum in this community, and their use is largely determined by the situation. Although Standard Mandarin is expected in many public domains, speakers' Mandarin carries local features, whether or not they are aware of them, including the mergers examined here. A self-reported frequency would therefore not reliably separate the two varieties. Consequently, we treat this score as an index of how much Mandarin is used overall.

Generation and Mandarin-QSM usage could in principle capture overlapping aspects of language background, as the older generation might also be more QSM-dominant. However, the correlation between the two across the 58 speakers was small and not reliable (r = .13, t(56) = 0.99, p = .33), and therefore both variables appear to capture distinct aspects of language background (and can safely be included simultaneously in the same regression model). Model. We fitted a single linear mixed-effects regression model, in which production accuracy was predicted from ABX perception accuracy, contrast, vowel, generation, and Mandarin-QSM usage. ABX accuracy was z-scored and the Mandarin usage score was meancentred before entering the model. The reference levels are the /f/∼/x/ contrast, the [a] context and the older generation.

The three linguistic predictors (abx accuracy, contrast, vowel) were entered in full-factorial form: all their pairwise and three-way interactions were included. The two sociolinguistic predictors were entered as two-way interactions with ABX accuracy and with contrast. We did not cross them with vowel or with each other because we are only interested in whether the sociolinguistic background shapes the perception-production link itself (i.e., how perception and contrast jointly predict production), rather than in whether it modulates vowel-context effects or whether the two aspects of sociolinguistic background interact .foot_5 lmer(prod_accuracy ~ abx_accuracy * contrast * vowel + abx_accuracy * contrast * generation + abx_accuracy * contrast * mandarin_usage + (1 + contrast | speaker) )

Given the larger number of inferential tests reported in this study and the largely exploratory nature of several comparisons, we used a more strict significance threshold (α = .01) to prevent reporting spuriously significant effects. Exact p-values are reported throughout. Effects with.01 ≤ p < .05 are noted, but are not treated as a reliable evidence of a difference.

3. Results

>Results

3.1. Data inclusion

The data of one participant was excluded due to difficulty reading the Chinese characters during the experiment. We also established an exclusion criterion based on ABX discrimination task performance according to a binomial test. Chance performance was defined as a success probability of 0.5. For each bilingual participant, we computed their accuracy on the control contrast (/m/∼/p/) and applied a one-sided binomial test against chance performance (50%), appropriate given the binary nature of the response (A or B). Participants whose accuracy was not significantly (with a significance threshold of 0.01) higher than chance were excluded from further analyses.

Under this criterion, a minimum of 31 correct responses out of 48 control trials were required for inclusion. Participants whose performance on the control contrast did not exceed this threshold were considered not to have reliably engaged with the task and were excluded from further analyses (N = 2). Following these exclusions, data from 58 participants were retained for analysis.

3.2. Perception results

Although participants completed the production task before the ABX discrimination task, we report the perception results first throughout the remainder of the article. This ordering reflects the analytical structure of the study rather than the chronological order of testing: the ABX measure serves both as a participant-level inclusion criterion and as a fixed-effect predictor in the perception-production link. Establishing the perception side first therefore allows the production analyses to draw on it as already-established.

The average ABX discrimination accuracy of the 58 retained bilingual QSM-Mandarin speakers was calculated for each contrast. Figure 2 shows the accuracy of the control contrast and the target contrasts. The mean ABX discrimination accuracy of the 58 retained QSM-Mandarin bilingual speakers was highest for the control contrast /m/∼/p/ (μ= 92.9%, sd = 7.8%). In contrast, discrimination accuracy was substantially lower for both target fricative contrasts. Performance for /f/∼/x/ was reduced (μ = 71.4%, sd = 17.0%), while accuracy for /s/∼/ù/ was lowest overall (μ = 58.8%, sd = 13.4%), approaching chance level. Performance for the control contrast was not reliably related to performance for the target contrasts (Spearman's ρ = .26, p = .049 for /f/∼/x/; ρ = .16, p = .22 for /s/∼/ù/, n = 58), so difficulty with the target contrasts cannot be attributed to general performance on the task. Thus, the participants who discriminated the control contrast well were not necessarily those who discriminated the target contrasts well.

The model results indicate that, relative to the control contrast /m/∼/p/, both target contrasts showed significantly reduced discrimination accuracy. Specifically, discrimination performance for /f/∼/x/ was significantly lower than for the control contrast (β = -2.27, SE = 0.21, z = -10.90, p < .001), and accuracy for /s/∼/ù/ was further reduced (β = -2.87, SE = 0.21, z = -13.57, p < .001), indicating a robust contrast-level effect on perceptual performance.

To further examine whether vowel context modulates ABX discrimination accuracy for both target contrasts, we fit an additional mixed-effects model to the target-only data, with contrast, vowel and their interaction as fixed effects. The model revealed a significant contrast × vowel interaction (β = 0.84, SE = 0.17, z = 4.95, p < .001), indicating that the effect of vowel context differed across the two contrasts. As shown in Figure 3, both target contrasts were discriminated more poorly in the [u] context than in the [a] context, but this reduction was substantially larger for /f/∼/x/ than for /s/∼/ù/.

3.3. Production results

Figures 4 and 5 display participant-level production accuracy for the /f/∼/x/ and /s/∼/ù/ contrasts in two vowel contexts, as rated by the L1 Mandarin speakers. Each point represents an individual speaker, plotted as production accuracy for one fricative against its paired category within the same contrast. The dashed diagonal line indicates equal production accuracy for the two categories.

For the /f/∼/x/ contrast (see Figure 4), most speakers cluster near the upper-right corner of the plot in both vowel contexts, indicating high production accuracy for both fricatives. A large proportion of points lie close to the diagonal in the top right corner, suggesting comparable (high) production accuracy for both /f/ and /x/ for many speakers. However, a subset of speakers shows an asymmetry, with high accuracy for /x/ but substantially lower accuracy for /f/, particularly in the [u] context.

A different pattern emerges for the /s/∼/ù/ contrast (see Figure 5). Points are more widely distributed across the production space, and many speakers deviate markedly from the diagonal. In particular, several speakers produce /s/ with high accuracy while showing substantially reduced accuracy for /ʂ/, resulting in points located far below the diagonal. This distribution indicates greater inter-speaker variability and stronger asymmetries between the two fricatives. Table 5 presents fixed-effect estimates from the mixed-effects logistic regression model predicting perceptual identification accuracy, with the fricative /f/ in the vowel context /a/ Figure 5. Participant-level production accuracy (based on L1 Mandarin speakers' perceptual evaluation) for the target contrast /s/∼/ù/ in both vowel contexts. The x-axis shows production accuracy (%) for one fricative, and the y-axis shows production accuracy (%) for its paired fricative within the contrast. The dashed diagonal line indicates equal accuracy for the two categories.

as the baseline. The model shows significant fricative × vowel interactions for all three contrasting fricatives (all p's < .001). The negative coefficients indicate that the [u] context disproportionately reduces identifiability for /s/, /ù/, and /x/ relative to /f/, with the largest asymmetry for /x/ (β = -3.48), followed by /s/ (β = -1.67) and /ù/ (β = -1.41). The negative coefficients indicate that the [u] context disproportionately reduces identifiability for /s/, /ù/, and /x/ relative to /f/, with the largest asymmetry for /x/ (β = -3.48), followed by /s/ (β = -1.67) and /ù/ (β = -1.41). Within each fricative, the [u] context lowers identifiability for /x/ (β = -2.29), /s/ (β = -0.48) and /ù/ (β = -0.22), but raises it for /f/ (β = 1.19). The effect is thus largest for /x/. Figure 4 shows that this difference mainly originates from the speakers with low /f/ accuracy, who produce almost no correct tokens before [a] but 20-40% of correct tokens before [u].

To check whether the speakers who merge one contrast are the same as those who merge the other, we computed the Spearman correlation between accuracy for /f/ and accuracy for /ù/. The two were not correlated across speakers (ρ = .10, p = .47, n = 58). We then took an accuracy below 20% as a descriptive threshold for near-complete merger. Seven speakers fell below this threshold for /f/, four of whom were also below it for /ù/. Merging one contrast therefore does not predict merging the other.

3.4. Perception and production link

A central goal of the present study was to determine whether perceptual discrimination ability predicts production accuracy within this bilingual community, and whether any such link is modulated by linguistic context: contrast and vowel, or by speakers' sociolinguistic background: generation and Mandarin-QSM usage. If perception and production are tightly linked, speakers with higher ABX accuracy should also produce the contrasts more accurately; if the phonetic or sociolinguistic factors reshape this mapping, that should surface as an interaction with ABX accuracy. We addressed these questions by stepwise backward elimination from a full interaction linear mixed-effects model including all three-way interactions among ABX accuracy, contrast, and each of the three remaining predictors (vowel, generation, and Mandarin-QSM usage), together with all corresponding lower-order terms and by-speaker random intercepts and random slopes for contrast (see the model detail above in Section 2.5). Production accuracy was calculated at the contrast level by averaging L1 Mandarin speakers' perceptual evaluation accuracy across the two phonemes within each contrast, yielding a value ranging from 0% to 100%. This aggregation ensured that the production measure corresponded directly to the perceptual discrimination task, which likewise evaluated contrast-level distinctions. So if perception and production are tightly coupled, higher perceptual discrimination accuracy should be associated with higher rated production accuracy. As reported in Table 6, none of the three three-way interactions reached the significance threshold (ABX accuracy × contrast × vowel: β = 0.25, p = .27; ABX accuracy × contrast × generation: β = -0.26, p = .20; ABX accuracy × contrast × Mandarin-QSM usage: β = -0.04, p = .29). At this stage of the analysis, only the three three-way interactions provide valid inferential tests of term inclusion. We therefore proceeded by dropping nonsignificant three-way interactions first, refitting, and evaluating the two-way interaction level of terms in turn, retaining α = .01 as the significance threshold at each step.

In the resulting two-way interaction model, none of the two-way interactions reached the threshold either (all p's > .1), and they were therefore dropped. A final inspection of the main-effects-only model showed that neither vowel nor Mandarin usage was significant, and these variables were therefore also removed. The terms that remained in the model after the stepwise procedure were contrast, generation, and ABX accuracy: the first two had associated p-values below .001, whereas the latter had a p-value above our significance threshold of α = .01. We nevertheless retained it in the model, as the purpose of this model was to investigate the relationship between perception and production.

The final model, was preferred over the full interaction model by both AIC and BIC, confirming that the dropped terms did not improve fit sufficiently to justify their complexity.

Its fixed-effects estimates are reported in Table 7. Contrast emerged as the single largest systematic predictor of production accuracy: /s/-∼/ù/ was produced less accurately than /f/∼/x/ (β = -0.30, SE = 0.02, p < .001). Generation was likewise significant, with older speakers producing both contrasts less accurately overall than younger speakers (β = -0.12, SE = 0.03, p < .001). ABX discrimination accuracy (i.e., perception) showed a positive relationship with production (β = 0.023, SE = 0.011, p = .038), but this effect did not reach significance (α = .01). Following the inferential convention set out above, we note this coefficient as a trend consistent with a weak positive perception-production relationship at the individual-differences level, but do not treat it as reliable evidence of such a relationship. Predicted production values from the final model, plotted as a function of (perceptual) ABX accuracy separately for each contrast, are shown in Figure 6.

In sum, our bilingual community shows robust contrast-level differences and a reliable generational difference in both perception and production accuracy, but no reliable individual-level relationship between ABX discrimination accuracy and production accuracy. The positive direction of the ABX coefficient in the final model is consistent with the expectations of perception-first accounts, but its magnitude does not meet the significance threshold we adopted for reliable inference. 7.

4. Discussion

This study investigated the relationship between speech perception and production in a bilingual community. Focusing on QSM-Mandarin bilinguals and two Mandarin fricative contrasts (/f/∼/x/ and /s/∼/ù/) previously reported to be variably realised, we asked whether perception and production remain systematically aligned at the individual level, and whether any such link is modulated by the contrast itself, the following vowel context, speaker generation, or degree of Mandarin-QSM usage in daily life. Our analyses produced two main findings. The first concerns differences between the two contrasts. The contrast /f/∼/x/ was perceived and produced more accurately than /s/∼/ù/, and the [a] context was easier than the [u] context for both contrasts. The contrast /s/∼/ù/ was also the site of substantially greater between-speaker variability in both perception and production.

The second finding concerns the relationship between perception and production. The two modalities agreed at the level of the phonological category: both ranked /s/∼/ù/ as harder than /f/∼/x/, and both ranked the [u] context as harder than [a]. At the level of the individual speaker, however, this agreement was weak. After accounting for contrast and generation, the coefficient for ABX discrimination accuracy in our final model was positive, in the direction predicted by perception-first theories, but did not reach the α = .01 threshold we adopted (p = .038).

4.1. Contrast-level asymmetry in perception and production

A central finding of the present study is the asymmetry between the /f/∼/x/ and /s/∼/ù/ contrasts. The contrast /f/∼/x/ was both more accurately discriminated and more reliably produced than /s/∼/ù/, and this pattern held coherently across both modalities. The sociolinguistic factors we examined further refine this picture. Generation and Mandarin-QSM usage each contributed to variation in both perception and production individually, with older speakers and speakers with lower Mandarin exposure showing reduced accuracy in both modalities.

The asymmetry can be understood in terms of three non-exclusive factors. The first is the way the two Mandarin contrasts map onto the QSM inventory. The second is the articulatory and acoustic difference between the two contrasts. The third is the different social meanings that variation in each contrast carries in the community.

As set out in Section 1.4, QSM lacks /f/, and Mandarin /f/ has a native anchor in QSM /h w /, while Mandarin /x/ is assimilated to QSM /h/. Before [a] the two therefore map onto distinct native forms, which PAM-L2 associates with accurate discrimination. Before [u], the rounding of the vowel imposes labialization on both and they converge on the same form. Both members of /s/∼/ù/ are assimilated to QSM /s/ in both contexts, since QSM has no retroflex category. The ordering we observe, with /f/∼/x/ discriminated and produced more accurately than /s/∼/ù/, is consistent with these mappings. We note, however, that the near-chance discrimination of /s/∼/ù/ reported here (58.8%) is closer to what Single-Category assimilation predicts than to the moderate discrimination associated with Category-Goodness assimilation. Since assimilation type is properly established through categorisation and goodness ratings rather than inferred from discrimination performance, we leave this question open and identify it as a target for future work in this community. Our results are therefore consistent with an extension of PAM-L2, initially developed for classroom L2 acquisition, to societal bilingualism in which two languages co-exist and interact, but a direct test of the model in this population further requires assimilation data.

The second factor is articulatory and acoustic. The two contrasts are not equally easy in their own right, independently of any assimilation pattern. The /f/ and /x/ differ in major place features (labiodental vs. velar constriction), producing substantial acoustic divergence, particularly in spectral energy distribution (Ladefoged & Maddieson, 1996). And the labial articulation of /f/ provides visible cues that may enhance both perceptibility and articulatory awareness (Peng, 1993; Rao & Shaw, 2021). The /s/ and /ù/ are both coronal fricatives distinguished primarily by anteriority and tongue configuration (alveolar vs. retroflex), producing more subtle spectral differences and lacking visually transparent articulatory cues (Jongman et al., 2000; Shadle, 1990). From this perspective, /f/∼/x/ may be more reliably maintained than /s/∼/ù/, not just because of its assimilation pattern but because the contrast itself is acoustically more salient and articulatorily more transparent.

The third factor is sociolinguistic. The two contrasts occupy markedly different positions in the social landscape of this community. The /f/∼/x/ merger is widely identified by speakers from other regions of China as a stereotypical feature of Fujian regional identity, and within the community it is also negatively evaluated as a marker of limited education (H. Lin, 2008). Speakers therefore might have an extralinguistic motivation to suppress the merger in their Mandarin productions, particularly in a relatively formal sentence reading context like our production task. Variation in /s/∼/ù/, by contrast, is commonly perceived as reflecting a broader southern accent, i.e., a regional feature without the same level of local stigma, and therefore does not carry the same corrective pressure as /f/∼/x/. Consequently, /f/∼/x/ may be more reliably maintained because speakers are socially motivated to maintain it, while /s/∼/ù/ variation persists because it lacks comparable social weight.

4.2. The relationship between perception and production

The second finding concerns the relationship between perception and production. Within this community, individual differences in ABX discrimination did not reliably predict indi-vidual differences in production accuracy for either contrast, and this nonsignificance held across vowel context, speaker generation, and Mandarin-QSM usage.

Several theoretical frameworks which differ in their premises, such as Motor Theory (Liberman & Mattingly, 1985) and Exemplar-based theory (Pierrehumbert, 2001), converge on one specific prediction: within a given population, the speakers whose perceptual categories are more differentiated should also be the speakers whose productions most clearly distinguish those categories. Our design was structured to test this prediction. We selected a bilingual community with substantial inter-speaker variation on the target contrasts, used dual-modality measures over the same segmental targets, and modelled both phonetic and sociolinguistic moderators of the relationship. Under these conditions, the within-community correlation between ABX accuracy and production accuracy was small and below our adopted significance threshold. This is a favourable setting in which to detect a within-individual coupling, but the positive link does not robustly emerge.

However, the present results should not be taken as evidence that perception and production are unrelated in principle. Both modalities track the same contrast asymmetry: /f/∼/x/ is more accurately perceived and produced than /s/∼/ù/, the same vowel context modulates difficulty in both tasks, and the same contrast concentrates the community's inter-speaker variability in both modalities. This parallelism is informative about which factors shape L2 phonological acquisition in this community, but it does not by itself constitute direct evidence of a perception-production link, because the same parallelism could equally arise from independent effects of those underlying factors on each modality. The more direct test of the link is at the level of individual differences: among speakers who share the same L1-L2 mapping and the same contrast, do those who perceive the contrast more accurately also produce it more accurately? It is at this level, where the prediction of perception-first frameworks is most directly tested, that our data fail to support it.

This pattern converges with prior work reporting weak, unstable, or task-dependent perception-production correlations at the level of individual differences (Baese-Berk, 2019; Martin, van Heugten, Kager, & Peperkamp, 2022; Melnik-Leroy et al., 2022; Turner, 2022a Turner, , 2022b)), and extends that literature into a contact setting in which, under perception-first assumptions, this type of alignment should have been most readily detectable (Best, 1995; Blevins, 2004; Liberman & Mattingly, 1985; Ohala, 1993). This lack of a link is also the pattern predicted by frameworks that treat perception and production as capable of changing independently within a bilingual speaker. The original SLM gave perception precedence over production (Flege, 1995). Its revision abandons this assumption. In the revised Speech Learning Model, L2 segmental perception and production "co-evolve without precedence" (SLM-r; Flege & Bohn, 2021, p. 29), a position motivated by inconsistent findings in L2 research and by evidence of a strong bidirectional connection between the two modalities. The Attrition and Drift in Access, Production, and Perception Theory (ADAPPT; de Leeuw & Chang, 2024) goes further. It rejects the assumption, shared by PAM-L2, the original SLM, and L2LP, that accurate perception must precede accurate production, and proposes that the two modalities recruit at least partly independent representations and need not change together within the same speaker. Our results, a group-level agreement with no reliable individual-level coupling, fit this stronger dissociation better than a model in which the two modalities co-evolve.

4.3. Limitations and future directions

Several limitations should be acknowledged. The first limitation in the present study concerns the nature of the measures used to operationalize perception and production. Production was assessed through intelligibility judgments by L1 Mandarin listeners, and perception through ABX discrimination accuracy. While this approach has the advantage of grounding both measures in functionally comparable, contrast-level performance, it reduces what are inherently gradient, temporally structured processes to categorical endpoints, a reduction that has important consequences for how the perception-production relationship can be detected and interpreted (Baese-Berk, Kapnoula, & Samuel, 2025).

On the perceptual side, ABX discrimination imposes a particular decisional format on the listener, one that emphasises categorical discrimination over graded perceptual sensitivity. A speaker whose ABX accuracy is moderate may nonetheless align with their own production on specific spectral or formant-transition cues that our contrast-level measure flattens. This is the perspective taken in work on cue weighting, which compares the weight an individual gives to a particular acoustic cue in perception with the weight given to the same cue in production (Schertz, Cho, Lotto, & Warner, 2015; Schertz & Clare, 2020). Comparisons of this kind can reveal individual-level correspondences that accuracy-based measures do not. On the production side, intelligibility judgments record whether the Mandarin listener heard the category intended by the bilingual speaker. However, two QSM-Mandarin bilinguals may produce, for example, /ù/ that L1 Mandarin listeners categorise correctly while using different lingual postures: one approximating the canonical Mandarin retroflex configuration, the other recruiting an alternative gesture that nonetheless produces an acoustically intermediate but identifiable output. These articulatory differences are largely invisible to listener-based intelligibility measures and might be only partially visible to acoustic analyses, since acoustic outputs aggregate over the gestural variation that produces them. These two reductions are, moreover, joined by a third: the relationship between perception and production should be understood as dynamically coupled rather than statically correlated, and our cross-sectional design cannot capture its temporal structure. Within perceptually grounded accounts of sound change (Blevins, 2004; Ohala, 1993), the two modalities are coupled through time but not necessarily in step at any given moment, and direct empirical work has shown that perceptual change can precede production change during an ongoing sound change (Kleber, Harrington, & Reubold, 2012; Pinget et al., 2020).

Each of these three reductions points to a complementary measurement direction. A more sensitive perceptual probe such as graded category goodness ratings or perceptionproduction imitation paradigms (Babel, McAuliffe, & Haber, 2013; Nielsen, 2011) would index a finer-grained dimension of perceptual acuity than ABX accuracy. Articulatory measures such as ultrasound tongue imaging, electromagnetic articulography, or real-time MRI could reveal the gestural strategies underlying perceptually equivalent productions. Longitudinal or finer-grained apparent-time designs would track how perception and production co-evolve along the trajectory of an ongoing change. Combined, these three extensions would directly address the limitations of the present cross-sectional, contrast-level design, and may reveal individual-level perception-production correspondences that our measures cannot.

One limitation concerns the comparison between our two measures. Perception was assessed with an ABX task using non-words, which measures discrimination without lexical support. Production was assessed with real words read aloud and then identified by Mandarin listeners, so that both the speaker and the listener could draw on lexical knowledge. The two measures therefore do not tap into the same level of processing. Both Melnik-Leroy et al. (2022) and Turner (2022a) show that an association between perception and production can be attenuated when the two are measured at different levels. The weak individual-level association reported here should be interpreted with this in mind. A design using the same material in both modalities would provide a more directly comparable test.

A further limitation concerns the role of social attitudes toward the two contrasts, which were not assessed in the present study. As noted in the introduction, the /f/~/x/ and /s/~/ù/ contrasts carry different social meanings within the Quanzhou community: the for-mer is overtly associated with regional Fujian identity and locally evaluated as a marker of limited Mandarin proficiency, while the latter reflects a broader southern accent pattern. These social evaluations may independently modulate both perceptual sensitivity and production behavior, above and beyond the effects of Mandarin exposure and generational experience examined here. Speakers with stronger awareness of the social meaning of a contrast may be more motivated to maintain or suppress it in production, and may attend more carefully to it in perception (cf. Eckert, 2018; Eckert & Labov, 2017). Without a measure of individual attitudes toward these contrasts, the present study cannot disentangle the effects of linguistic experience from those of social evaluation on the perception-production relationship. Future work incorporating attitudinal measures would allow a more complete picture of the sociolinguistic factors shaping phonological contrast variation in this community.

Speech style is a final consideration. The present study elicited production data in a single, relatively formal sentence reading task, which represents a narrow slice of the range of styles in which speakers actually use these contrasts in daily life. There is well-established evidence that phonological contrast maintenance varies with speech style: speakers may maintain a contrast more carefully in formal speech than in spontaneous conversation, where reduction and coarticulation are more likely to produce merger-like outputs (cf. Labov, 1972 Labov, , 1994)). This style-dependence means that the perception-production relationship observed here may not generalise to more naturalistic speech contexts. In particular, speakers who show high production accuracy in the reading task may exhibit considerably more variation in spontaneous speech, where social and interactional pressures on style-shifting are more active. Future work should examine the perception-production relationship across a wider range of speech styles, including spontaneous conversation and interactive tasks, to assess the robustness of the present findings.

5. Conclusion

This study examined perception and production of two Mandarin fricative contrasts in a societal bilingual community undergoing increasing Mandarin dominance. Across QSM-Mandarin bilingual speakers, we observed a clear asymmetry: the /f/∼/x/ contrast was both better discriminated and more accurately produced than the /s/∼/ù/ contrast. This ordering is consistent with the way the two contrasts map onto the QSM inventory, and is further consistent with acoustic, articulatory, and sociolinguistic differences between the two contrasts.

At the level of individual differences, however, the correlation between perceptual and production accuracy was small and below our adopted (conservative) significance threshold, and was not reliably modulated by contrast type, vowel context, generational experience, or relative Mandarin-QSM usage. These findings indicate that perception and production agree at the group level, with both modalities ranking the same contrasts and contexts as harder, but not at the individual level: a speaker who perceives a contrast more accurately is not reliably the speaker who produces it more accurately. This within-individual alignment is the central prediction of perception-first frameworks of L1 and L2 phonology, and our data do not robustly support it at the contrast-level we measured. Whether this reflects a genuine limit on the prediction or the limits of our measurement strategy is an open question that future work, using finer perceptual and articulatory probes will need to settle.

A. Acoustic grounding of L1-Mandarin perceptual judgements

This appendix reports the acoustic validation summarised in Section 2.5: the betweencategory Euclidean-distance analysis that shows L1-Mandarin listeners' identification accuracy is anchored in the acoustic properties of the bilingual productions.

Acoustic measurements

The first four spectral moments (CoG, spectral SD, skewness, kurtosis) were computed from the central 80% of each fricative using the time-averaged DFT procedure described in Weng et al. (2026), with the window and overlap constraints proposed by DiCanio (2021); Shadle, Chen, Koenig, and Preston (2023). Every moment was then z-scored across all tokens in the corpus.

For each contrast we restricted the acoustic space to the two moments reported to be primary for that pair. /f/ and /x/ are separated mainly on CoG and spectral SD (Rao & Shaw, 2021); /s/ and /ù/ are separated mainly on CoG and skewness (Jongman et al., 2000; Li et al., 2014). For each speaker × vowel × contrast cell, we computed the mean z-scored moments per fricative and took the Euclidean distance between the two fricative centroids in the relevant 2-D space. This yields one distance value per cell, at the same grain as the L1-judged identification accuracy used in Section 2.5.

Results

Fitting a binomial mixed-effects model of L1-judged accuracy on acoustic distance and its interaction with contrast, with by-speaker random intercept and contrast slope, we found that greater between-category acoustic distance predicted higher L1-judged identifiability in both contrasts. The results showed that the acoustic distance × contrast interaction reached the significance threshold (β = +0.39, p = .007), indicating that the gain in L1-judged identifiability per unit of between-category acoustic separation was not the same in the two contrasts. Decomposing the interaction by contrast, the slope of accuracy on distance was positive and reliable in both pairs, but steeper for /s/∼/ù/ (β = +1.09, p < .001) than for /f/∼/x/ (β = +0.70, p < .001); greater between-category acoustic distance therefore predicted higher identifiability in both contrasts.

The authors have no competing interests to declare.

Assimilation type and goodness of fit are established empirically, by asking listeners to identify each non-native sound as the closest native category and to rate it as an exemplar of that category(Best & Tyler, 2007).

The present study did not include such a task. The mappings proposed below are hypotheses based on the two inventories, and we return to this point in Section 4.3. We thank an anonymous reviewer for drawing our attention to this and for proposing the alternative classifications considered here.2 Whether this mapping is better described as Single-Category or as Category-Goodness assimilation is not settled by the inventories alone. Both Mandarin sibilants are assimilated to QSM /s/, but Mandarin /s/ is plausibly a closer match to it than Mandarin /ù/ is, which would make the mapping one of Category-Goodness and would predict moderate rather than poor discrimination. We thank an anonymous reviewer for this suggestion. The two classifications agree on the ordinal expectations derived here, and we return in Section 4 to the question of which of them is more consistent with the discrimination data.

The production task was administered before the ABX discrimination task by design. The ABX trials present participants with canonical L1 Mandarin productions of the target fricatives, and we wanted to ensure that the bilinguals' own productions were not primed or restructured by recent exposure to those tokens. Running production first therefore preserves the speakers' baseline production patterns under unprompted conditions and protects the production data from short-term perceptual-learning effects.

We are aware that non-words are frequently used to assess lexical knowledge in alphabetic languages, and numerous influential studies have explored comparisons between real-word and non-word stimuli (e.g., English; Hay, Drager, and Thomas 2013; French; Melnik-Leroy, Turnbull, and Peperkamp 2022). Unlike alphabetic systems, however, written Chinese is often classified as morpho-syllabic: each character typically represents a monosyllabic morpheme and is associated with both a syllable (pronunciation) and a meaning. Because of this dual mapping, the distinction between "real-word" and "non-word" is less straightforward than in alphabetic languages (since the construction of "non-words" must be done through the combination of real-word syllables). For this reason, this study focuses on real-word stimuli in the reading task.

glmer(correct ∼ fricative × vowel + (1 + fricative × vowel | speaker) + (1 + fricative × vowel | rater), family = binomial)

Since production accuracy is a proportion and therefore bounded between 0 and 1, we also refitted the final model with an ordered beta distribution, which is defined on the unit interval and accommodates values at the bounds, using glmmTMB(Brooks et al., 2017). As the estimates and the conclusions are unchanged, we report this model in the Supplementary Materials.
